```yaml
type: topic_bank_longform
bank_id: mf-2026-09-25
topics:
  - id: L-001
    title: The Psychology of People Who Prefer Being Alone
    framing: identity
    target_min: 3.5
    cold_open: They cancel plans and feel relieved. They're not lonely — and here's what's actually going on.
    chapters:
      - title: Solitude is not loneliness
        concept: Chosen solitude vs unwanted isolation
      - title: They recharge differently
        concept: Stimulation and social energy differences (introversion)
      - title: They're selective, not cold
        concept: Quality over quantity in relationships
      - title: They think better alone
        concept: Solitude supports reflection and creativity
      - title: When alone time becomes avoidance
        concept: Healthy solitude vs withdrawal; signs to watch
    closing_idea: Being alone is a skill. The goal is choosing it, not hiding in it.
    related_shorts:
      - S-001
      - S-004
    cta_question: Do you recharge alone or with people?
    priority: 1
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 507
      cold_open: They cancel plans and feel relieved. They're not lonely. And what's happening in their head is more interesting than you think.
      chapters:
        - title: Solitude is not loneliness
          narration: Psychologists draw a sharp line between solitude and loneliness. Loneliness is the painful gap between the connection you want and the connection you have. Solitude is time alone that you choose. The difference is control. Someone who prefers being alone isn't missing people. They're choosing when to see them. That's why the same empty evening can feel peaceful to one person and crushing to another. It isn't the empty room that hurts. It's whether you picked it. Think of the person who spends Sunday reading, walking alone, cooking for one, and ends the day feeling full, not empty. Nothing is missing for them.
        - title: They recharge differently
          narration: "Social time costs energy for everyone, but not equally. People higher in introversion tend to feel drained sooner by noise, crowds and constant conversation, and they recover in quiet. People higher in extraversion often feel the opposite, energized by company. Neither is better. It's closer to a different battery design. So when someone leaves a party early, it may not mean they had a bad time. Their battery just emptied faster than yours. A useful way to see it: extraverts often think out loud, while many introverts think first and speak later. Both are simply different routes to the same answers."
        - title: They're selective, not cold
          narration: "People who like being alone are often described as cold. Usually, they're selective. Because social time is expensive for them, they spend it carefully, on a few people who really matter. Ask about their friends and you'll often hear about three people, not thirty. Those few relationships tend to run deep: long conversations, real honesty, less small talk. Quantity was never the goal. Quality was. You'll also notice they tend to remember the details you told them months ago. When your attention goes to fewer people, each person gets more of it."
        - title: They think better alone
          narration: "There's a practical benefit too. Without other people to monitor and respond to, attention turns inward. That's where reflection happens: sorting through experiences, making sense of feelings, connecting ideas that never meet in a noisy room. Many writers, scientists and artists deliberately protect time alone for exactly this reason. For them, solitude isn't empty time. It's where the thinking gets done. Even a short walk alone after a hard conversation can help your mind file what happened, instead of carrying it around all day."
        - title: When alone time becomes avoidance
          narration: "But here's an honest caveat. Solitude is healthy when it's chosen and you come back from it refreshed. It becomes a warning sign when it turns into avoidance: staying in because people feel threatening, or cutting everyone off after being hurt. A simple test is this. Does time alone leave you restored, or just hidden? If it's hiding, that's worth talking about with someone you trust. Healthy solitude usually sits alongside a few warm relationships. It's the balance that matters, not the number of hours alone."
      closing: Being alone is a skill. The goal isn't to never need people. It's to choose solitude, not hide in it.
    status: unused
    video_id: null
    published_at: null
  - id: L-002
    title: 7 Brain Glitches You Experience Every Day
    framing: self_callout
    target_min: 3.9
    cold_open: Your brain glitches dozens of times a day. Here are seven you've definitely felt.
    chapters:
      - title: Déjà vu
        concept: Familiarity signal firing without a real memory
      - title: Phantom phone vibration
        concept: Expectation-driven false alarms
      - title: Tip of the tongue
        concept: "Partial retrieval: meaning found, word not"
      - title: Say a word until it sounds fake
        concept: Semantic satiation
      - title: You learn a word, then see it everywhere
        concept: Frequency illusion (Baader-Meinhof)
      - title: The falling jolt as you sleep
        concept: Hypnic jerk
      - title: The song stuck in your head
        concept: Earworms / involuntary musical imagery
    closing_idea: These glitches are shortcuts your brain uses to be fast. Occasionally, fast is wrong.
    related_shorts:
      - S-016
      - S-013
    cta_question: Which glitch happens to you most?
    priority: 1
    notes: "7 chapters at ~70–85 words each to stay within the 5-minute cap. Hypnic jerk explanation: describe current theories as not fully settled."
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 565
      cold_open: Your brain glitches dozens of times a day. You've felt every one of these. Here are seven, and what's actually going on.
      chapters:
        - title: Déjà vu
          narration: One. Déjà vu, that sudden feeling you've lived this exact moment before. Researchers think it happens when your brain's sense of familiarity fires without a real memory behind it. One part says 'I know this,' while another part correctly knows you don't. That mismatch is the eerie feeling. It seems more common when you're tired or stressed, and it's usually harmless. It usually lasts only a few seconds, which is part of why it feels so strange.
        - title: Phantom phone vibration
          narration: Two. Phantom phone vibration. Your pocket buzzes, you check, and there's nothing. Surveys suggest most regular phone users have felt it. Your brain is constantly listening for that signal, so it's primed to say yes to anything similar, like a muscle twitch or a brush of fabric. It's a false alarm from a brain that decided missing a message is worse than checking for nothing. Try leaving your phone in another room for an hour. Many people notice the phantom buzz even more.
        - title: Tip of the tongue
          narration: "Three. The word on the tip of your tongue. You know what it means, maybe even the first letter, but the word won't come. Memory stores meaning and sound somewhat separately, and here your brain found the meaning but can't reach the sound. The frustrating part: trying harder often makes it worse. Let it go, and it tends to arrive on its own. Another trick: think of something related, like where you heard the word, and the connection often pulls it up."
        - title: Say a word until it sounds fake
          narration: Four. Say a word over and over, spoon, spoon, spoon, and it starts to sound fake. That's semantic satiation. Rapid repetition seems to temporarily tire out the link between a word's sound and its meaning. For a moment, it's just noise. Give it a minute, and the word goes back to normal. Researchers have even used it to help people with speech anxiety, repeating a feared word until it loses its sting.
        - title: You learn a word, then see it everywhere
          narration: Five. You learn a new word, or notice a new car model, and suddenly it's everywhere. That's the frequency illusion, also called the Baader-Meinhof phenomenon. It didn't become more common. Your attention just started flagging it, and your brain mistakes 'noticed more' for 'happening more.' The same bias can make a rare event feel common right after you hear about it on the news.
        - title: The falling jolt as you sleep
          narration: Six. You're drifting off to sleep and suddenly jolt awake, sometimes with a feeling of falling. That's a hypnic jerk, and most people get them. Scientists aren't fully sure why. One idea is that as your muscles relax, your brain briefly misreads it as falling and jerks you awake. Caffeine, stress and tiredness seem to make them more frequent. They're considered normal and are rarely a sign of anything serious.
        - title: The song stuck in your head
          narration: "Seven. The song stuck in your head. Psychologists call it involuntary musical imagery, better known as an earworm. Catchy, repetitive tunes are the usual culprits, especially ones you heard recently or only partly. One odd fix has some research behind it: chewing gum, which seems to interfere with the inner rehearsal that keeps the loop playing. Another fix people report: listen to the whole song once, so your brain gets its ending."
      closing: Every one of these is a shortcut your brain uses to be fast. Most of the time, fast is right. Occasionally, it glitches.
    status: published
    video_id: 0ZfD57Ab8bc
    published_at: 2026-09-26T14:00:00.000Z
  - id: L-003
    title: Why Kind People Attract Selfish People
    framing: relationship
    target_min: 3.2
    cold_open: If you keep ending up with people who take and never give, it isn't bad luck.
    chapters:
      - title: Over-giving signals availability
        concept: Takers seek low-resistance sources
      - title: The hard 'no'
        concept: Difficulty setting boundaries; guilt
      - title: Keeping score silently
        concept: Unspoken expectations lead to resentment
      - title: Why takers stay
        concept: Relationships persist when one side absorbs all cost
      - title: The reset
        concept: Small boundaries, noticing who stays
    closing_idea: Kindness is not the problem. Unconditional access is.
    related_shorts:
      - S-003
      - S-023
    cta_question: Have you ever been the 'always available' friend?
    priority: 1
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 455
      cold_open: If you keep ending up with people who take and never give, it isn't bad luck. There's a pattern here, and you can change it.
      chapters:
        - title: Over-giving signals availability
          narration: "Kind people tend to say yes quickly. They lend, listen, show up, cover for others, and rarely ask for anything back. To most people, that's simply generous. But to someone who mostly takes, it's a signal: here's a person who won't push back. Takers aren't always scheming. They just drift toward whoever offers the least resistance, and an always-available person offers none at all. You might notice it at work first: the person who always stays late ends up with everyone else's tasks, simply because they never refused."
        - title: The hard 'no'
          narration: For many kind people, 'no' feels physically uncomfortable. It comes with guilt, with fear of seeming selfish, with worry about letting someone down. So they say yes and absorb the cost instead. The problem is that people learn from what you allow. Every unwilling yes teaches the other person that your limits are negotiable, and the requests quietly grow. It helps to remember that a clear, kind 'no' is also a form of respect. It tells people exactly where they stand.
        - title: Keeping score silently
          narration: Here's the part kind people rarely admit. Even while giving freely, they're often keeping a silent score. Not out of pettiness, but out of hope that the effort will someday be noticed and returned. When it isn't, resentment builds. And because that expectation was never said out loud, the other person has no idea there's anything owed at all. The healthier move is to say the expectation early, while it's still small, instead of letting it pile up into a bill no one knew about.
        - title: Why takers stay
          narration: A relationship can last a long time when one person carries all the cost. From the taker's side, nothing is wrong. They get support, attention and help, with no demands. There's no reason for them to change, or to leave. The imbalance only hurts one person, and that person is usually too kind to say so. That's why these relationships rarely end with a big fight. They end when the giver finally runs out.
        - title: The reset
          narration: "The fix isn't becoming cold. It's adding small boundaries. Say 'I can't this time' once, and see what happens. Say what you need plainly, instead of hoping someone guesses. Then watch. The people who respect it are worth keeping. The ones who disappear were there for your availability, not for you. You'll likely feel guilty at first. That guilt isn't proof you're wrong. It's just the feeling of breaking an old habit. A good question to ask yourself: if I stopped offering, how many of these relationships would still be there in a month?"
      closing: Kindness is not the problem. Unconditional access is.

  - id: L-004
    title: The Psychology of People Who Don't Post Online
    framing: identity
    target_min: 3.2
    cold_open: They see everything you post. You haven't seen a post from them in years.
    chapters:
      - title: Privacy as control
        concept: Keeping ownership over self-presentation
      - title: Less need for outside approval
        concept: Internal vs external validation
      - title: Observers, not performers
        concept: Audience vs actor roles online
      - title: Opting out of comparison
        concept: Reducing social comparison exposure
      - title: What it doesn't mean
        concept: Not posting isn't automatically secure or mysterious
    closing_idea: Silence online can be a choice about attention, not a lack of a life.
    related_shorts:
      - S-018
    cta_question: When did you last post?
    priority: 2
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 454
      cold_open: They see everything you post. You haven't seen a post from them in years. So what's actually going on?
      chapters:
        - title: Privacy as control
          narration: Posting online means handing strangers a version of yourself to judge. For some people, that feels like giving up control. By not posting, they keep complete ownership of how they're seen. People get to know them in person, on their terms, not through a highlight reel they'd have to maintain. It's the difference between being known by a few people and being watched by many. Their private life isn't hidden. It's just not on display.
        - title: Less need for outside approval
          narration: Likes and comments work like small public rewards. Some people rely on them a lot, others barely notice them. People who don't post often seem less driven by that outside approval. Whether their day went well doesn't depend on who reacted to it. That doesn't make them better, but it does make them harder to rattle. They'll happily share good news with friends in person. They just don't need a public audience to feel it counted. Research on approval-seeking suggests the less your mood rides on reactions, the steadier it tends to be.
        - title: Observers, not performers
          narration: Online, there are performers and there's an audience. Many non-posters are firmly in the audience. They scroll, they read, they notice who's doing what. They just don't feel the pull to step on stage. That's why they sometimes know surprising details about your life while sharing almost nothing about theirs. Some of them are the best listeners you'll meet, because they're used to taking in far more than they give out. Ask them about a group chat drama and they'll often know every detail, having never said a word.
        - title: Opting out of comparison
          narration: "Social media is a comparison machine: everyone's best moments, all day long. Research links heavy social comparison with lower mood for many people. Some non-posters step back for exactly this reason. Not posting keeps them off the scoreboard entirely, which can feel like a quiet relief. It also frees up time. Hours that might go into curating posts go into hobbies, people, or rest instead. They still see the highlight reels, of course. They just aren't adding their own to the pile."
        - title: What it doesn't mean
          narration: One honest warning. Not posting isn't automatically a sign of confidence or mystery. Some people avoid posting because they're anxious about judgment, or going through a hard time. Silence online has many possible reasons. The only way to know which one applies is to actually talk to them. If someone in your life has gone quiet online recently, a simple message asking how they are costs nothing. Assumptions are easy. A real conversation tells you far more than any profile ever could.
      closing: Silence online can be a choice about attention, not proof of an empty life.

  - id: L-005
    title: The Psychology of People Who Never Ask for Help
    framing: identity
    target_min: 3.3
    cold_open: They'll help you move house at 6 a.m. They'll never ask you for anything.
    chapters:
      - title: Learned self-reliance
        concept: Early experience that asking led nowhere
      - title: Fear of being a burden
        concept: Overestimating the cost to others
      - title: Help feels like losing control
        concept: Autonomy and control needs
      - title: People say yes more than you think
        concept: People underestimate how willing others are to help
      - title: How to start asking
        concept: Small, specific requests
    closing_idea: Letting people help you is also a gift to them.
    related_shorts:
      - S-019
    cta_question: When did you last ask for help?
    priority: 2
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 467
      cold_open: They'll help you move house at six in the morning. They will never ask you for anything. Here's why.
      chapters:
        - title: Learned self-reliance
          narration: "For many people who never ask for help, it started early. Maybe asking led to disappointment, criticism, or nothing at all. So they learned a simple rule: it's faster and safer to do it yourself. That rule worked back then, and it quietly followed them into adult life, long after the original reason disappeared. You'll often see it in the eldest sibling, or anyone who had to grow up quickly and manage things on their own. Being capable became part of their identity, so needing help can feel like losing a piece of who they are."
        - title: Fear of being a burden
          narration: The most common thought is 'I don't want to be a burden.' People who think this tend to overestimate how much their request would cost someone, and how annoyed that person would feel. So even small asks, a lift, a favor, some advice, feel like imposing. They'd rather struggle than risk being too much. Ironically, the same person would happily help you with exactly what they're too shy to ask for themselves. They'll often apologize before asking, or wrap the request in so many excuses that it barely sounds like one.
        - title: Help feels like losing control
          narration: "Help can also feel like losing control. If someone else does it, they might do it differently, or you might owe them. For people who value independence strongly, accepting help can feel like admitting weakness. Doing everything alone keeps life predictable, even when it's exhausting. It shows up in small ways: refusing a hand with heavy bags, or working late instead of asking a colleague for five minutes. The irony is that trying to carry everything alone often leads to burnout, which ends up costing everyone more."
        - title: People say yes more than you think
          narration: Here's where research is clear. In studies by Francis Flynn and Vanessa Bohns, people asking strangers for help consistently underestimated how many would say yes, often by around half. Most people are more willing than we assume. And being asked often makes people feel trusted, not burdened. Think about the last time someone asked you for help. You probably felt useful, not annoyed. They feel the same. In other words, the fear of being a burden is usually far bigger than the actual burden.
        - title: How to start asking
          narration: "So start small and specific. Not 'I'm struggling,' but 'Can you look at this for ten minutes?' Specific requests are easier to say yes to, and each one builds the evidence your brain needs: asking went fine. Over time, the old rule starts to loosen. It also gives the other person a chance to show up for you, which is how close relationships are built. Notice how people respond. Most of the time, they'll be glad you asked."
      closing: Letting people help you isn't weakness. It's also a gift to them.

  - id: L-006
    title: 7 Signs You're an Overthinker — and What It Says About You
    framing: identity
    target_min: 3.2
    cold_open: You've replayed today three times already. And it's not even night yet.
    chapters:
      - title: You replay conversations
        concept: Rumination
      - title: Small decisions take forever
        concept: Decision paralysis / maximizing
      - title: You plan for the worst
        concept: Catastrophizing as preparation
      - title: You need more information first
        concept: Intolerance of uncertainty
      - title: You can't enjoy the moment
        concept: Attention pulled to past and future
      - title: Thinking feels like control
        concept: Analysis as a safety behavior
      - title: How to switch off
        concept: Scheduled worry time, action over analysis
    closing_idea: Overthinking is a careful mind with no off switch. You can build one.
    related_shorts:
      - S-010
      - S-015
    cta_question: Which sign hit hardest?
    priority: 2
    notes: Supportive tone; no clinical diagnosis language. 7 chapters at ~70–85 words each.
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 467
      cold_open: You've replayed today three times already, and it isn't even night yet. Here are seven signs of an overthinker, and what they really say about you.
      chapters:
        - title: You replay conversations
          narration: "One. You replay conversations. What you said, how they looked, what you should have said instead. Psychologists call this rumination: going over the same material without reaching anything new. It feels like problem-solving, but mostly it keeps the problem alive. One simple check: if you've thought about it three times and nothing new came up, you're ruminating, not solving."
        - title: Small decisions take forever
          narration: Two. Small decisions take forever. Which restaurant, which shirt, which reply. Researchers describe some people as maximizers. They want the best option, not just a good one. Maximizers tend to search longer and end up less satisfied, because there's always another option they didn't pick. Setting a rule like 'good enough in ten minutes' can save hours on choices that barely matter.
        - title: You plan for the worst
          narration: "Three. You plan for the worst. Before a trip or a meeting, you've already imagined everything that could go wrong. It feels like preparation. But imagining disasters triggers real stress, so you end up living through bad outcomes that never actually happen. Try asking one extra question: what's the most likely outcome, not the worst one?"
        - title: You need more information first
          narration: Four. You need more information before you act. One more review, one more opinion, one more search. This links to what psychologists call intolerance of uncertainty, the feeling that not knowing is unbearable. The catch is that certainty rarely arrives, so action keeps getting delayed. Deciding with most of the information, then adjusting, usually beats waiting for all of it.
        - title: You can't enjoy the moment
          narration: Five. You can't fully enjoy the moment. You're at a great dinner, but half your mind is on tomorrow, or on something from last week. Attention pulled toward the past and future leaves less for the present, and the present is where good moments actually happen. Even naming five things you can see around you can pull your attention back into the room.
        - title: Thinking feels like control
          narration: Six. Thinking feels like control. Stopping the analysis feels like letting your guard down. For many overthinkers, the thinking itself becomes a safety habit. It feels protective, but it rarely changes the outcome. It mostly changes how tired you are. Noticing that pattern is the first step to loosening it.
        - title: How to switch off
          narration: "Seven, and this is the good news. Overthinking responds to practice. One technique is scheduled worry time: fifteen minutes a day when you're allowed to worry, and outside of it, you postpone the thought. Another is choosing one small action instead of another round of analysis. Action gives your brain the certainty that thinking never could. Most overthinkers are thoughtful, careful and deeply caring. The goal isn't to stop thinking. It's to choose when."
      closing: Overthinking is a careful mind with no off switch. The good news is, you can build one.
    status: in_progress

  - id: L-007
    title: "Chanakya vs Modern Psychology: 6 Rules That Still Work"
    framing: borrowed
    target_min: 3.4
    cold_open: Two thousand years ago, one strategist wrote rules about human nature. Psychology keeps proving him right.
    chapters:
      - title: Keep your plans quiet
        concept: Announcing goals can create a premature sense of achievement
      - title: Test people in hard times
        concept: Costly signals reveal true commitment
      - title: Don't be too predictable
        concept: Predictability invites exploitation
      - title: You become your company
        concept: Social contagion of habits and emotions
      - title: Face what you fear
        concept: Exposure reduces fear; avoidance grows it
      - title: Desire has no finish line
        concept: Hedonic adaptation
    closing_idea: "Old wisdom, new evidence: human nature hasn't changed much."
    related_shorts:
      - S-026
    cta_question: Which rule do you already follow?
    priority: 2
    notes: Many quotes online are wrongly attributed to Chanakya. Present each as a theme 'attributed to Chanakya Niti', no verbatim quotes, and pair with the modern concept.
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 486
      cold_open: More than two thousand years ago, a strategist in ancient India wrote about human nature. Modern psychology keeps landing in the same place. Here are six ideas attributed to Chanakya, and what research says.
      chapters:
        - title: Keep your plans quiet
          narration: "The first idea attributed to Chanakya: keep your plans to yourself. Research adds a twist. In studies by Peter Gollwitzer, people who announced certain identity goals, like becoming a lawyer, sometimes worked less on them afterward. Telling others can give you an early sense of already being that person, and the drive to act drops. Share progress, not intentions. So the next time you're tempted to announce a big plan, try working on it quietly for a month first."
        - title: Test people in hard times
          narration: "Second: judge a friend in hard times. Psychologists and economists talk about costly signals, behavior that's believable precisely because it costs something. Saying 'I'm there for you' costs nothing. Showing up during a crisis costs time, effort and convenience. That's why hardship reveals who's genuine. It's the moment loyalty stops being free. Pay attention to who calls when things go wrong, not just who shows up to celebrate when things go right."
        - title: Don't be too predictable
          narration: "Third: don't be too straight. A saying attributed to Chanakya compares the overly straight to trees that get cut down first. The lesson isn't to become dishonest. It's that complete predictability can be exploited. If people always know you'll say yes, give in, or stay silent, some will plan around it. Honesty is a strength. Total predictability is a weakness. A little unpredictability, like not agreeing to every request instantly, protects your time without harming anyone."
        - title: You become your company
          narration: "Fourth: you become the company you keep. Research on social contagion supports this. Habits and moods, even behaviors like smoking, tend to spread through social networks. The people around you quietly set your normal: what feels acceptable, ambitious or excessive. Choosing your company is choosing your defaults. If you want to change a habit, spending more time with people who already have that habit is one of the easiest levers you have."
        - title: Face what you fear
          narration: "Fifth: face fear rather than run from it. This is one of psychology's most reliable findings. Avoiding what frightens you brings short-term relief but teaches your brain the fear was justified, so it grows. Gradual exposure does the opposite. Each time you face it and come through fine, the fear loses ground. Start with the smallest version of what scares you, and build up step by step."
        - title: Desire has no finish line
          narration: "Sixth: desire has no finish line. Psychologists call it hedonic adaptation. A raise, a new phone, a bigger house. Each brings a burst of happiness, then it becomes normal and the goalpost moves. That doesn't make ambition bad. It means expecting any one thing to make you permanently happy is a trap. Gratitude for what you already have is one of the few reliable ways researchers have found to slow that adaptation down."
      closing: Old ideas, new evidence. Two thousand years later, human nature hasn't changed much.

  - id: L-008
    title: The Psychology of People Who Stay Calm in Chaos
    framing: identity
    target_min: 3
    cold_open: Everyone's panicking. One person is quietly handling it. Here's what's different in their head.
    chapters:
      - title: They reframe the situation
        concept: Cognitive reappraisal
      - title: They focus on what they control
        concept: Internal locus of control
      - title: They calm the body first
        concept: Slow breathing and physiological regulation
      - title: They accept uncertainty
        concept: Tolerance of uncertainty
      - title: It's trained, not born
        concept: Calm as a practiced skill
    closing_idea: Calm isn't the absence of fear. It's what you do with it.
    related_shorts:
      - S-006
    cta_question: Are you the calm one or the panicker?
    priority: 3
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 427
      cold_open: Everyone's panicking. One person is quietly handling it. Here's what's different inside their head.
      chapters:
        - title: They reframe the situation
          narration: "First, calm people change how they read the situation. Psychologists call it cognitive reappraisal. Instead of 'this is a disaster,' it becomes 'this is a problem with steps.' The facts stay the same, but the emotional reaction shrinks. Research consistently finds reappraisal is one of the healthier ways to manage emotions. A quick way to try it: describe the problem out loud as if you were advising a friend. It works best when it's honest. Calm people don't pretend things are fine; they just describe them more usefully."
        - title: They focus on what they control
          narration: "Second, they quickly sort what they can control from what they can't. This links to what psychologists call an internal locus of control, the belief that your actions matter. Calm people spend their energy on the next useful move, not on what's already out of their hands. A simple question helps: what's one thing I can do in the next ten minutes? Worrying about what you can't change drains energy you need for what you can."
        - title: They calm the body first
          narration: "Third, they calm the body first. Panic is partly physical: racing heart, fast shallow breathing. Slow breathing, especially longer exhales, helps shift the nervous system toward calm. A steadier body sends a steadier signal back to the brain, and clear thinking comes back online. One easy pattern: breathe in for four counts and out for six, repeated a few times. It sounds too simple to work, but the body often leads and the mind follows."
        - title: They accept uncertainty
          narration: Fourth, they accept not knowing how it ends. Much of panic is the demand for certainty right now. Calm people tolerate uncertainty. They act on the best information available and adjust as things change, instead of freezing until everything is clear. They'll often say 'Here's what we know so far,' which calms everyone around them too. Making a choice with incomplete information is uncomfortable. Calm people are simply more used to that discomfort.
        - title: It's trained, not born
          narration: "And the most important part: calm is trained, not born. Emergency workers, pilots and surgeons practice under pressure until the response becomes automatic. You can do a smaller version in everyday stress: breathe, reframe, take the next step. Each small crisis handled well makes the next one feel familiar. The next time something goes wrong, treat it as practice. That mindset alone is part of what makes calm people calm. Nobody is calm in every situation. Calm people have just had more practice with the situations that shake them."
      closing: Calm isn't the absence of fear. It's what you do with it.
    status: unused
    video_id: null
    published_at: null
```

```yaml
  - id: S-036
    title: 4 Smart Psychology Tricks That Actually Work
    framing: self_callout
    format: rapid_list
    length_s: 33
    concept: "Benign everyday influence: Ben Franklin effect, strategic silence, name use, and giving a reason ('because')"
    hook: Four small psychology tricks that make conversations go your way.
    beats_hint: "Ethical, everyday. 1) Ask for advice, not help (Ben Franklin effect). 2) Stay silent after a question. 3) Use their name once, early. 4) Give a reason with 'because'. Closer: use them kindly."
    cta_question: Which trick are you trying first — 1, 2, 3 or 4?
    tag_line: Send this to someone with an interview this week.

    notes: Tests the trending keyword 'smart psychology' (vidIQ showed +2.1k%). 'Because' = Langer 1978, works mainly for small requests; Ben Franklin effect evidence is modest — keep claims soft. Excludes mirroring and reciprocity.
    visual_brief:

        - phone call at a desk

    script:
```

```yaml
    framing: self_callout

    concept: "Present bias (temporal discounting): distant deadlines feel abstract, so near-term comfort wins until the deadline makes the cost feel real"
    hook: You had two weeks. You started last night. Your brain planned that.
    beats_hint: "Future discounted; comfort now feels real; urgency only when close; reframe: make the future feel near with tiny next-day deadlines."
    cta_question: When did you actually start your last big task?
    tag_line: Send this to your last-minute friend.

    notes: Present bias is well supported. Don't equate procrastination with laziness or any diagnosis. Framed around work, not money (WealthPsyche covers money).
    visual_brief:
      setting_arc:

        - bedroom with laptop

      must_show:

        - a sticky note with a small deadline for tomorrow
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 63
      loops_into_hook: true
      beats:
        - beat: hook
          line: You had two weeks. You started last night. Your brain planned that.
        - beat: mechanism
          line: Your brain discounts the future. A deadline two weeks away feels abstract, while relaxing right now feels real.
        - beat: mechanism
          line: That's present bias. Only when the deadline gets close does the cost finally feel urgent enough to act.
        - beat: reframe
          line: The fix isn't guilt. Make the future feel near with tiny deadlines for tomorrow. Because

  - id: S-045
    title: 3 Psychology Tricks to Stop Overthinking
    framing: self_callout
    format: rapid_list
    length_s: 28
    concept: Affect labelling, scheduled worry time, and cognitive defusion reduce rumination
    hook: Your brain won't stop replaying it. These three tricks actually turn the volume down.
    beats_hint: "1) Name the feeling out loud (affect labelling). 2) Give worry a fixed 15-minute slot. 3) Prefix with 'I'm having the thought that…' (defusion). Closer: thoughts are weather, not facts."
    cta_question: Which trick will you try tonight — 1, 2 or 3?
    tag_line: Send this to someone who's awake at 2 AM thinking.
    priority: 1
    notes: Evidence-based self-help (affect labelling, worry postponement, ACT defusion), not therapy. No 'cure' or medical claims. 'Calms' not 'switches off'.
    visual_brief:
      setting_arc:
        - bedroom at night
        - balcony in the evening
        - desk with a notebook
      must_show:
        - someone lying awake
        - a phone alarm labelled 'worry time'
        - writing a thought in a notebook
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 65
      loops_into_hook: true
      beats:
        - beat: hook
          line: Your brain won't stop replaying it. These three tricks actually turn the volume down.
        - beat: item
          line: One. Name it. Saying 'I'm anxious about tomorrow' out loud calms the brain's alarm.
          on_screen: 1 · Name it
        - beat: item
          line: Two. Give worry a slot. Fifteen minutes at six. Outside that, you postpone it.
          on_screen: 2 · Worry slot
        - beat: item
          line: Three. Add 'I'm having the thought that…'. It puts distance between you and the story.
          on_screen: 3 · Step back
        - beat: closer
          line: Thoughts are weather, not facts. And next time
    seo:
      description: |-

  - id: S-046
    title: Why Traffic Turns Calm People Angry
    framing: borrowed
    format: single
    length_s: 25
    concept: "Frustration–aggression plus anonymity: blocked goals and faceless drivers lower restraint"
    hook: The calmest person you know becomes a different human the moment traffic stops moving.
    beats_hint: "Blocked goal + lost control → anger; anonymity inside a car → others become obstacles; reframe: they're stuck and late too."
    cta_question: Are you calm in traffic? Honest answer.
    tag_line: Send this to the friend who honks the most.
    priority: 2
    notes: Frustration–aggression (Dollard; revised by Berkowitz). Keep claims soft. Indian city traffic visuals.
    visual_brief:
      setting_arc:
        - city traffic jam inside a car
        - auto-rickshaw queue
        - arriving at the office
      must_show:
        - gridlock
        - a hand on the horn
        - the other driver's face shown as a tired human
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 59
      loops_into_hook: true
      beats:
        - beat: hook
          line: The calmest person you know becomes a different human the moment traffic stops moving.
        - beat: mechanism
          line: Traffic blocks your goal and takes away control. Blocked goals are one of the fastest routes to anger.
        - beat: mechanism
          line: Add anonymity. Inside a car, other drivers aren't people with faces, just obstacles, so restraint drops.
        - beat: reframe
          line: "Next jam, remember: they're stuck too, and they're late too. Even"
    seo:

  - id: S-050
    title: Why Sunday Evenings Feel So Heavy
    framing: self_callout
    format: single
    length_s: 27
    concept: "Anticipatory anxiety: the brain rehearses Monday's stress before it arrives (the 'Sunday scaries')"
    hook: It's Sunday, six p.m. Nothing is wrong, but your chest already feels like Monday.
    beats_hint: "Brain rehearses tomorrow; weekend with no clear ending makes the switch sudden; reframe: a small Sunday closing ritual."
    cta_question: Do you get the Sunday scaries? Yes or no.
    tag_line: Send this to your Monday-dreading friend.
    priority: 1
    notes: Must go public on a Sunday. 'Sunday scaries' is informal; anticipatory anxiety is the real term. No clinical claims.
    best_day: sun
    visual_brief:
      setting_arc:
        - living room on a Sunday evening
        - balcony with tea
        - laptop bag by the door
      must_show:
        - a clock or phone showing Sunday 6 PM
        - a work notification preview
        - writing a short list as a closing ritual
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 63
      loops_into_hook: true
      beats:
        - beat: hook
          line: It's Sunday, six p.m. Nothing is wrong, but your chest already feels like Monday.
        - beat: mechanism
          line: That's anticipatory anxiety. Your brain starts rehearsing tomorrow's stress, so you feel the week before it begins.
        - beat: mechanism
          line: It often hits hardest when the weekend has no clear ending, and the switch to Monday feels sudden.
        - beat: reframe
          line: "Give Sunday a small closing ritual: plan one thing, then enjoy one thing. Because"
    seo:
```

```yaml
    length_s: 31
    concept: "Social judgement findings: warmth judged before competence (Fiske), follow-up questions increase liking (Huang 2017), humblebragging backfires (Sezer 2018), spontaneous trait transference (Skowronski 1998)"
    hook: People decide who you are fast. These four psychology facts show how.
    beats_hint: "1) Warmth before competence. 2) Follow-up questions make you more likeable. 3) Humblebrags backfire. 4) Gossip about flaws gets attached to you. Closer: now you know the rules."

    tag_line: Send this to someone with a big meeting tomorrow.

        - networking event

    script:

        - beat: hook
          line: People decide who you are fast. These four psychology facts show how.

          line: One. People judge your warmth before your competence. Kind comes first, smart comes second.

        - beat: item
          line: Two. Asking follow-up questions makes people like you more than telling great stories.
          on_screen: 2 · Ask more
        - beat: item
          line: Three. Humble-bragging backfires. People like a plain brag more than a fake complaint.
          on_screen: 3 · No humblebrag
        - beat: item

          on_screen: 4 · Gossip sticks
        - beat: closer

  - id: S-055
    title: 3 Psychology Tricks Used on You Every Day
    framing: relationship
    format: rapid_list
    length_s: 27
    concept: "Everyday compliance and framing techniques: door-in-the-face, labelling (altercasting), and attribute framing"
    hook: These three psychology tricks get used on you daily, and you rarely notice.
    beats_hint: "Defensive. 1) Huge ask first, then the 'reasonable' one (door-in-the-face). 2) Labelling you 'helpful' to get help. 3) Framing: 90% fat-free vs 10% fat. Closer: now you'll catch them."
    cta_question: Which trick has been used on you — 1, 2 or 3?
    tag_line: Send this to someone who always ends up saying yes.
    priority: 1
    notes: "Template '[N] Psychology Tricks' (outliers: MindSnacks 129x breakout, Fairy Mindset). Don't copy 'People Rarely Notice'. Avoid excluded tactics (foot-in-the-door, reciprocity, scarcity, anchoring, social proof). Non-money examples so it doesn't overlap WealthPsyche."
    visual_brief:
      setting_arc:
        - office cabin
        - family living room
        - supermarket aisle
      must_show:
        - a colleague making a huge request, then a smaller one
        - someone saying 'you're always so helpful' with a hopeful smile
        - two food labels side by side
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 61
      loops_into_hook: true
      beats:
        - beat: hook
          line: These three psychology tricks get used on you daily, and you rarely notice.
        - beat: item
          line: One. They ask for something huge first, so the smaller request suddenly feels reasonable.
          on_screen: 1 · Big ask first
        - beat: item
          line: Two. 'You're always so helpful.' Once labelled helpful, you feel pressure to prove it.
          on_screen: 2 · The label
        - beat: item
          line: Three. 'Ninety percent fat-free' sounds healthier than 'ten percent fat', though it's identical.
          on_screen: 3 · The frame
        - beat: closer
          line: Now you'll catch them. Every time, because
    seo:

  - id: S-056
    title: 4 Psychology Tricks for Reading Anyone
    framing: relationship
    format: rapid_list
    length_s: 35
    concept: "Practical people-reading: establish a baseline, recurring topics reveal values, first-person pronoun use and status (Kacewicz 2014), how they talk about absent people"
    hook: Reading people isn't magic. It's four simple psychology tricks most people never use.
    beats_hint: "1) Know their normal first (baseline). 2) What they keep bringing up = what they value. 3) Less power, more 'I'. 4) How they talk about absent people. Closer: practise one this week. No eye contact, micro-expressions, crossed arms, mirroring or first impressions (all excluded)."
    cta_question: Which trick will you try first — 1, 2, 3 or 4?
    tag_line: Send this to someone who's great at reading people.
    priority: 1
    notes: Fits the 'how to read people' / 'psychology tricks' clusters. Item 4 is a heuristic, keep 'often'. Not a lie-detection video.
    visual_brief:
      setting_arc:
        - office meeting
        - café with friends
        - family dinner
      must_show:
        - a person behaving differently from their usual self
        - someone steering conversation back to one topic
        - a person talking about someone who just left the room
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 82
      loops_into_hook: true
      beats:
        - beat: hook
          line: Reading people isn't magic. It's four simple psychology tricks most people never use.
        - beat: item
          line: One. Know their normal first. A change from their usual behaviour tells you more than any single gesture.
          on_screen: 1 · Their normal
        - beat: item
          line: Two. Notice what they keep bringing up. People circle back to what they care about most.
          on_screen: 2 · Repeat topics
        - beat: item
          line: Three. Count the 'I's. In conversations, people with less power often say 'I' more.
          on_screen: 3 · Count the I's
        - beat: item
          line: Four. Watch how they talk about absent people. That's often how they'll talk about you.
          on_screen: 4 · Absent people
        - beat: closer
          line: Practise one this week. And remember,
    seo:
      description: |-
        4 psychology tricks for reading anyone.
        Know their baseline, track repeat topics, count the I's, and listen to how they talk about others.

        Which trick will you try first — 1, 2, 3 or 4?

  - id: S-057
    title: 3 Dark Psychology Facts About Normal People
    framing: identity
    format: rapid_list
    length_s: 25
    concept: "Everyday dark-side findings: moral licensing (Merritt 2010), diffusion of responsibility (Darley & Latané), just-world belief and victim blaming (Lerner)"
    hook: These three dark psychology facts describe normal people. Maybe even you.
    beats_hint: "1) Doing good licenses a little bad. 2) Crowds dilute responsibility. 3) Victim-blaming protects the belief in a fair world. Closer: uncomfortable but true."
    cta_question: Which one have you caught yourself doing?
    tag_line: Send this to someone who loves dark psychology.
    priority: 2
    notes: "Compact version (~25s) of the 'Dark Psychology Facts' cluster (18% hit rate). Descriptive, not cynical. Don't depict violence; for #3 show a neutral example (e.g. someone judged for being scammed)."
    visual_brief:
      setting_arc:
        - office pantry
        - busy street
        - family WhatsApp group on a phone
      must_show:
        - someone rewarding themselves with junk food after a gym session
        - a crowd walking past someone who dropped their things
        - relatives judging someone who got scammed
    script:
      source: bank
      word_count: 58
      loops_into_hook: true
      beats:
        - beat: hook
          line: These three dark psychology facts describe normal people. Maybe even you.
        - beat: item
          line: One. After doing something good, people feel licensed to do something slightly bad.
          on_screen: 1 · Moral licence
        - beat: item
          line: Two. In a crowd, everyone waits for someone else to help. Responsibility gets diluted.
          on_screen: 2 · Nobody helps
        - beat: item
          line: Three. People blame victims to keep believing the world is fair and they're safe.
          on_screen: 3 · Blaming victims
        - beat: closer
          line: Uncomfortable, right? But it's true, because
    seo:
```
